=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a grid with H rows and W columns.
Let (i,j) denote the cell at the i-th row from the top and the j-th column from the left.
There are N horizontal bars numbered from 1 to N placed on the grid.
Bar i consists of L_i blocks of size 1 \times 1 connected horizontally, and its leftmost block is initially at cell (R_i, C_i).
That is, initially, bar i occupies the cells (R_i, C_i), (R_i, C_i + 1), \dots, (R_i, C_i + L_i - 1).
It is guaranteed that there is no cell occupied by two different bars.
The current time is t = 0.
At every time t = 0.5 + n for some non-negative integer n, the following occurs in order of i = 1, 2, \dots, N:

- If bar i is not on the bottom row (the H-th row), and none of the cells directly below the cells occupied by bar i is occupied by any bar, then bar i moves down by one cell. That is, if at that time bar i occupies the cells (r,C_i),(r,C_i+1),\dots,(r,C_i+L_i-1)\ (r < H), and the cell (r + 1, C_i + j) is not occupied by any bar for all j (0 \leq j \leq L_i - 1), then bar i now occupies (r + 1, C_i), (r + 1, C_i + 1), \dots, (r + 1, C_i + L_i - 1).
- Otherwise, nothing happens.

Let (R'_i, C_i), (R'_i, C_i + 1), \dots, (R'_i, C_i + L_i - 1) be the cells occupied by bar i at time t = 10^{100}. Find R'_1, R'_2, \dots, R'_N.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W N
R_1 C_1 L_1
R_2 C_2 L_2
\vdots
R_N C_N L_N

Output

Print N lines.
The i-th line (1 \leq i \leq N) should contain R'_i.

Constraints


- 1 \leq H, W \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq R_i \leq H
- 1 \leq C_i \leq W
- 1 \leq L_i \leq W - C_i + 1
- In the initial state, there is no cell occupied by two different bars.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 4 4
1 2 3
3 2 2
2 1 2
2 4 1

Sample Output 1

2
4
3
4

The following three diagrams represent the grid at times t = 0, 1, and 2 from left to right.
Colored rectangles represent the bars, and the number inside each rectangle indicates its bar number.

The changes in the grid state are explained as follows:

- At t = 0.5:
- i = 1: The cells directly below bar 1 are (2,2),(2,3),(2,4). Among these, (2,2) is occupied by bar 3 and (2,4) is occupied by bar 4, so nothing happens.
- i = 2: The cells directly below bar 2 are (4,2),(4,3), which are not occupied by any other bar, so bar 2 moves down by one cell.
- i = 3: The cells directly below bar 3 are (3,1),(3,2), which are not occupied by any other bar, so bar 3 moves down by one cell.
- i = 4: The cell directly below bar 4 is (3,4), which is not occupied by any other bar, so bar 4 moves down by one cell.


- At t = 1.5:
- i = 1: The cells directly below bar 1 are (2,2),(2,3),(2,4), which are not occupied by any other bar, so bar 1 moves down by one cell.
- i = 2: Bar 2 is on the bottom row, so nothing happens.
- i = 3: The cells directly below bar 3 are (4,1),(4,2). Among these, (4,2) is occupied by bar 2, so nothing happens.
- i = 4: The cell directly below bar 4 is (4,4), which is not occupied by any other bar, so bar 4 moves down by one cell.



At times t = 2.5, 3.5, \dots, there is no bar such that the cells directly below it are all unoccupied, so nothing happens. Thus, the grid at time t = 10^{100} is the same as at t = 2 (the rightmost diagram above).
Therefore, R'_1 = 2, R'_2 = 4, R'_3 = 3, R'_4 = 4.

Sample Input 2

382 382 3
3 3 3
8 8 8
2 2 2

Sample Output 2

382
382
381

4 8 2
1 2 2
2 5 3
5 4 3
4 5 2
1 5 2

Sample Output 3

5
5
4
3
5
4
2

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

We can implement iterative lazy segment tree? Range assign and range min. Simpler recursive:
```
class SegTree:
    def __init__(self,n,inf):
        self.n=n
        self.inf=inf
        self.mn=[inf]*(4*n+5)
        self.lazy=[None]*(4*n+5)  # None means no pending assignment
    def apply(self,idx,val):
        self.mn[idx]=val
        self.lazy[idx]=val
    def push(self,idx):
        if self.lazy[idx] is not None:
            self.apply(idx*2,self.lazy[idx])
            self.apply(idx*2+1,self.lazy[idx])
            self.lazy[idx]=None
    def pull(self,idx):
        self.mn[idx]=min(self.mn[idx*2], self.mn[idx*2+1])
    def assign(self,idx,l,r,ql,qr,val):
        if ql<=l and r<=qr:
            self.apply(idx,val); return
        self.push(idx)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        if ql<=mid: self.assign(idx*2,l,mid,ql,qr,val)
        if qr>mid: self.assign(idx*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr,val)
        self.pull(idx)
    def query(self,idx,l,r,ql,qr):
        if ql<=l and r<=qr: return self.mn[idx]
        self.push(idx)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        res=self.inf
        if ql<=mid: res=min(res,self.query(idx*2,l,mid,ql,qr))
        if qr>mid: res=min(res,self.query(idx*2+1,mid+1,r,ql,qr))
        return res
```

Actually bar3 row4, bar2 below row3 empty -> moves to3. bar3 below row5 empty -> moves to5. t2.5: bar1 blocked by bar2 row3? bar2 at row3; bar2 blocked by bar3 row5? below row4 empty -> moves to4. bar3 bottom. t3.5: bar1 blocked by bar2 row4; bar2 blocked by bar3 row5 -> blocked. Final bar1 row3, bar2 row4, bar3 row5. Yes.

- What if lower bar has final row not simply bottom due to another lower bar; chain works.

Now, is q = min cover value over interval always the final row of the lowest (highest up) obstacle? Let's prove by induction on R descending. Suppose all bars with initial row > R_i have final rows according to formula and are inserted. For bar i, any bar k with R_k > R_i and I_k∩I_i≠∅ is processed and has final r_k. The segment tree cover_min[c] = min r_k over processed bars covering c. Query q = min_{c∈I_i} cover_min[c] = min r_k over all processed bars intersecting I_i. If no such, q=INF. Claim final r_i = H if q=INF else q-1. Need show process reaches that.

We can argue by considering the "lowest" (minimum row) obstacle among all bars initially below i. Let q be min final row of such obstacles. If none, i falls to bottom H. If exists, let k be an obstacle with final row q. Since k is below i and intersects, i cannot pass row q-1? Actually i cannot move to row q because at row q-1, the cell below in the intersecting column is occupied by k at row q, so blocked. Could i be blocked even higher by some other bar? That other bar would have final row < q, contradicting q min. Could i be blocked lower? It can move down until row q-1. Need ensure there is no temporary obstruction that prevents it from reaching q-1. Any bar that intersects i and is below i has final row >= q. At final, if a bar has final row s > q, it occupies row s. For i to move from row t to t+1, it needs row t+1 empty in all columns of I_i. It might be blocked temporarily by a bar that later moves down; but eventually all bars settle. We need show i reaches q-1. Since all obstacles with final row > q will move away from rows above q? Actually if an obstacle j has final row s > q, it may at some time occupy row q+1 or above, blocking i temporarily. But j will eventually move down to s, clearing the way. Since process repeats indefinitely, after all lower bars reach final positions, i can move down until blocked by the min obstacle. But what if i is above some lower bar j with final row s > q, and j is blocked by i? No, lower bar j doesn't depend on i. It will reach final position regardless. So eventually lower bars settle. Then i can fall. However, there is a subtlety: i itself may block a lower bar? No, lower bar's downward movement doesn't require cells above it. But if i is directly above j and j wants to move down, j's below cells are below j, not i. So i doesn't block j. Thus lower bars settle independently of i. Good. So by induction, after sufficiently many steps, all lower bars final. Then i moves down one step at a time until row q-1. At row q-1, below includes k at row q, so blocked. It cannot move further. So final r_i=q-1. If no lower bars, after all lower none, i falls to bottom. This proof assumes lower bars settle independently, which follows from induction bottom-up because their final positions depend only on even lower bars. Good.

Now, process order by index not row: Does lower bar settle independently of upper bars? We argued yes. But in actual process, upper bars are processed before/after lower bars each time. Could an upper bar occupy a cell below a lower bar? No, upper remains above. Could an upper bar prevent a lower bar from moving by occupying a cell directly below lower bar? To occupy below lower bar, upper would need be below lower, impossible. So lower bar's movement condition only checks cells below itself, which are never occupied by upper bars (since upper bars are above). What about a bar with disjoint interval but same columns? If disjoint, no. Thus lower bars' trajectories are independent of all bars above them. This is key. So bottom-up induction valid.

Let's verify with index order: Suppose lower bar has large index, upper bar small index. At a time step, upper bar processed first. It may move down into a cell that is below lower bar? No, upper is above lower, so moving down one cell could move into row just above lower or maybe into lower's row if lower moved? If lower hasn't moved yet and is at row r_l, upper at r_l-1, upper's below includes lower's row, so blocked. It cannot move into lower's row. If lower is at row r_l and upper at r_l-2, upper moves to r_l-1, still above. So never below. Thus lower's below cells unaffected. Good.

Thus algorithm correct.

Now, let's consider if q = INF but there are lower bars with intervals not intersecting? They don't matter. q INF means no lower bar covers any column in I_i. Then i falls to bottom H. But wait, could a lower bar with disjoint interval at final row H be in a column adjacent? No direct block. It falls to bottom. Good.

Now, segment tree range assign to v. Since v = q-1, and q is min over interval, v < q. But what if q=INF=H+1, v=H. All values in interval are INF=H+1, so v < all. Good. If q finite, all values >= q > v. So assignment to v is valid. If there are positions with value INF and q finite? q is min, so if q finite, no INF in interval? Wait if some position has INF, then min would be INF? No min of finite and INF is finite, so q finite. There can be INF positions in interval? If any position INF, min would be INF? Actually min(finite, INF) = finite. So q finite even if some positions INF. Example interval [1,2], cover_min[1]=5, cover_min[2]=INF, q=5. Then v=4. All values in interval are >=5? cover_min[2]=INF=H+1, which is > v. Yes assignment sets INF to 4. So all positions become v. Good.

Need ensure INF > H+1? If INF=H+1, and v=H, INF > v. Good. If H=2e5, INF=200001.

Now, segment tree implementation details with lazy assignment:
- We can initialize mn all INF, lazy maybe -1? But values can be 1..H, so lazy=0 means no pending? Since assignment values >=1. Use 0 as no lazy. But if H could be 0? no. So lazy array of int 0 = no pending. apply sets lazy[idx]=val. push if lazy[idx]!=0.
- However, if val=0 never used. Good.
- mn initial INF.
- In apply, mn[idx]=val, lazy[idx]=val.
- In push, if lazy[idx]!=0: apply children, lazy[idx]=0.
- In pull, mn[idx]=min(mn[left], mn[right]).
- query partial: push before recursing to ensure children mn correct. But if we don't push, children mn may be stale if parent has lazy. Since query partial needs children values; push propagates. Good.
- assign partial: push before recursing; after children updated, pull.

Potential recursion limit: depth ~18, no issue, but number of recursive calls maybe high. Set recursionlimit 1e6 anyway.

Can optimize by using iterative segment tree? Let's evaluate performance. 2e5 bars, each query + assign. Recursive function calls ~ maybe 2*2e5*O(log W) ~ 7.2M? Actually each operation visits O(log W) nodes but with partial overlaps maybe up to 4log. Query and assign each ~ O(log W) with recursive calls. 2e5*2*~70 = 28M calls? Might be high but still maybe okay in Py if optimized? Need be careful for AtCoder (likely 2 sec? But Python allowed maybe 2 sec? We want efficient).

Let's design a faster iterative lazy segment tree or use a different data structure.

Because update is range assignment and query range min. There is an iterative segment tree with lazy propagation (AtCoder lazy segtree) that can do both in O(log N) with arrays and loops, faster than recursion. We can implement.

AtCoder LazySegTree:
- size = 1 << ceil_pow2(n)
- data = [INF]*(2*size)
- lazy = [0]*size (0 no-op)
- op = min, e = INF
- mapping: f(x) = val if f!=0? Actually assignment: mapping(f, x) = f if f != 0 else x. But since assignment value can be 0? no. We can use lazy=0 as no-op.
- composition: g after f? If we apply assignment f then g, result g. So composition(g, f)=g if g!=0 else f.
Need implement functions:
```
def _all_apply(k, f):
    data[k] = f
    if k < size: lazy[k] = f
def _push(k):
    f = lazy[k]
    if f:
        _all_apply(2*k, f)
        _all_apply(2*k+1, f)
        lazy[k] = 0
```
Range apply:
```
def range_apply(l,r,f): # [l,r) 0-index
    if l==r: return
    l += size; r += size
    l0=l; r0=r
    for i in range(log,0,-1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: _push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: _push((r0-1) >> i)
    while l < r:
        if l & 1: _all_apply(l, f); l += 1
        if r & 1: r -= 1; _all_apply(r, f)
        l >>= 1; r >>= 1
    for i in range(1, log+1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: _update(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: _update((r0-1) >> i)
```
Range prod:
```
def range_prod(l,r):
    if l==r: return INF
    l += size; r += size
    for i in range(log,0,-1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l: _push(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r: _push((r-1) >> i)
    sml=INF; smr=INF
    while l < r:
        if l & 1: sml = min(sml, data[l]); l += 1
        if r & 1: r -= 1; smr = min(data[r], smr)
        l >>= 1; r >>= 1
    return min(sml, smr)
```
This is standard. Need update ancestors after range_apply. Since assignment can overwrite, update uses min children.

But we can simplify because range assignment always sets all elements in range to same value. The iterative lazy segtree is robust.

Need convert 1-indexed inclusive [C, C+L-1] to 0-index half-open [C-1, C+L-1). Query and assign same interval.

Let's implement AtCoder style carefully.

Pseudo:
```
class LazySegTree:
    def __init__(self, n, inf):
        self.n = n
        self.inf = inf
        self.size = 1
        self.log = 0
        while self.size < n:
            self.size <<= 1
            self.log += 1
        self.data = [inf] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * self.size  # 0 means no assignment
    def _update(self, k):
        self.data[k] = self.data[2*k] if self.data[2*k] < self.data[2*k+1] else self.data[2*k+1]
    def _all_apply(self, k, f):
        self.data[k] = f
        if k < self.size:
            self.lazy[k] = f
    def _push(self, k):
        f = self.lazy[k]
        if f:
            self._all_apply(2*k, f)
            self._all_apply(2*k+1, f)
            self.lazy[k] = 0
    def range_apply(self, l, r, f):
        if l >= r: return
        l += self.size
        r += self.size
        l0, r0 = l, r
        for i in range(self.log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                self._push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                self._push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                self._all_apply(l, f)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                self._all_apply(r, f)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        for i in range(1, self.log + 1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                self._update(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                self._update((r0 - 1) >> i)
    def range_min(self, l, r):
        if l >= r:
            return self.inf
        l += self.size
        r += self.size
        for i in range(self.log, 0, -1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                self._push(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                self._push((r - 1) >> i)
        sml = self.inf
        smr = self.inf
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                if self.data[l] < sml: sml = self.data[l]
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                if self.data[r] < smr: smr = self.data[r]
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        return sml if sml < smr else smr
```
Need ensure lazy array size = size (only internal nodes). In _all_apply, if k < size set lazy[k]=f. But in range_apply, _all_apply may be called on leaf k >= size; no lazy. Good.

In range_apply, after applying to nodes, update ancestors. The conditions for updating ancestors are standard. Need check if l0/r0 conditions correct. AtCoder code:
```
for i in range(1, log+1):
    if ((l >> i) << i) != l: update(l >> i)
    if ((r >> i) << i) != r: update((r-1) >> i)
```
But l and r have been modified? In AtCoder, they save l2=l, r2=r before loop, then use l2/r2 for push and update. My code uses l0,r0 for push and update, good. For range_min, no update after.

Potential issue with lazy values: assignment value f can be 0? No, rows >=1. INF maybe H+1. So lazy=0 no-op safe. If H=0 no.

Let's test iterative segtree mentally with n=4, all INF. range_assign(0,2,5):

while: l=4,r=6. l even, r even. l=2,r=3. r&1 -> r=2, _all_apply(2,5) sets node2 (covering [0,2)) data=5 lazy=5. l=1,r=1 end.

Works.

But note in range_apply, if assignment f equals current data? It still sets. Fine.

Could we avoid iterative complexity and use recursive? Iterative code is a bit long but acceptable. Need ensure no bugs.

Alternative: Since range assignment always sets to a value lower than all current values in interval, we can use a segment tree with "range assign" and "range min" but maybe a simpler "segment tree beats" not needed. Iterative lazy is good.

Let's consider memory: data size 2*size <= 2*2^18? W up to 2e5, size=262144, data 524288 ints, lazy 262144 ints. Fine.

Now, sorting bars by R descending. Need output original order. We can store ans array length N. For each bar in sorted order:
```
bars = [(R,C,L,idx) for idx in range(N)]
bars.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)
for R,C,L,idx in bars:
    l = C-1
    r = C+L-1  # exclusive? If C 1-index, C+L-1 inclusive, exclusive = C+L-1? Let's compute: 0-index left = C-1, right inclusive = C+L-2, exclusive = C+L-1. Yes r_excl = C + L - 1.
    q = seg.range_min(l, r_excl)
    if q == INF: row = H
    else: row = q - 1
    ans[idx] = row
    seg.range_apply(l, r_excl, row)
```
Need if q could be INF? INF=H+1. If q=H+1, row=H. If q finite, row=q-1. Could q be H+1 but row=H, and later query q=H, row=H-1. Good.
What if q=0? no.

Now, let's revisit the reduction to range assignment. We should ensure when processing a bar, the segment tree stores for each column the minimum final row among processed bars covering that column. When we assign interval to row, we set all columns in interval to row. But what if some column in interval already has a smaller value from a processed bar? We argued impossible because q is min over interval, and row=q-1 < q <= all values. So row is smaller than every value in interval. Thus assignment is correct. This relies on row = q-1, not row = min(H, q-1)? If q=INF, row=H < INF. If q finite, row=q-1 < q. So yes.

But wait: What if q = 1? Then row=0, invalid. But can q=1? A processed bar has final row at least its initial row R_k. Since R_k > R_i >=1, R_k >=2. Also final row >= R_k? Actually bars only move down, so final row >= initial row. So processed bar final row >= R_k >=2. Thus q>=2. So row>=1. Good. If H=1, no processed bars with R>R_i because R_i>=1 and R_k>1 impossible, so q=INF. Good.

Now, let's think about initial no overlap guarantee. Our algorithm doesn't check overlaps. If initial overlaps, process invalid, but constraints avoid.

Potential issue: The problem says "At every time t = 0.5 + n for some non-negative integer n, the following occurs in order of i = 1,2,...,N". At each such time, bars move based on current positions after previous bars in same time. We considered lower bars settle independently. But let's rigorously prove lower bars independent of upper bars in terms of final positions. Is it possible that an upper bar, by moving down, occupies a cell that is directly below a lower bar? Wait "directly below a lower bar" means row = r_lower + 1. Upper bar is above lower bar, so its row < r_lower. It moves down to row +1 <= r_lower. It can never reach r_lower+1 because to do so it would need pass through r_lower, occupied by lower bar or if lower moved, still upper remains above lower? Let's track if lower moves down, upper could move into lower's old row r_lower, but lower new row > r_lower, so upper row = r_lower < lower row. It never exceeds lower row. Thus upper never occupies row below lower. So lower's below cells are safe. What if intervals are disjoint, upper could be in a column not overlapping lower, but then not directly below lower anyway. So yes.

What about a bar with initial row below but not intersecting upper? It is independent of upper. So bottom-up.

What about three bars where an upper bar with small index moves down and blocks a middle bar's downward movement? Middle bar's downward movement checks below itself; upper bar is above middle, cannot be below. So no.

Thus final positions independent of index. Good.

Now, let's think about the segment tree update: We assign row to entire interval. Suppose there are processed lower bars covering columns in I_i with values > row. After assignment, those columns' cover_min becomes row, representing that bar i is now the highest (smallest row) bar covering those columns. This is correct because bar i's final row is above all lower bars. For future upper bars, the obstacle with minimum row in those columns is bar i, not the lower bars. So storing min row is sufficient. Good.

Now, let's consider if a future upper bar j intersects I_i and also a lower bar k with smaller row than i? Wait if k is lower than i, row_k > row_i. The min row among bars covering a column is row_i (smaller). So j will be blocked by i first. Good.

Now, let's think about the "time = 10^100" maybe not enough if there are many bars moving in a chain one step per time? Actually a chain of N bars can move one cell per time step? Let's see stack of bars all same column, bottom at H-1? If bottom moves one step per time, above follows next time, so a top bar might take N time steps to move one? Wait if all bars in a vertical stack with empty space below, at each time step bottom moves down, then the one above moves into bottom's old cell in same time step? Because process order matters. If indices from top to bottom? Let's simulate stack of 3 at rows 1,2,3, H=5, indices top to bottom (1,2,3). t0.5: bar1 blocked by bar2, bar2 blocked by bar3, bar3 moves to4. t1.5: bar1 blocked by bar2, bar2 moves to3? Wait bar3 at4, bar2 below row3 empty, so moves to3. bar3 moves to5. t2.5: bar1 moves to2? bar2 at3, below row2 empty? Actually bar1 at1, below row2 empty (bar2 at3), so moves to2. bar2 blocked by bar3 at5? below row4 empty, moves to4. t3.5: bar1 blocked by bar2 at4? bar1 at2, below row3 empty? Wait bar2 at4, so row3 empty, bar1 moves to3. bar2 blocked by bar3 at5. t4.5: bar1 blocked by bar2 at4? bar1 at3, below row4 occupied, blocked. Final rows 3,4,5. Top moved from1 to3 over 4 time steps. Total time steps ~H? Actually each bar moves H-R_i times, but top moved 2 steps over 4 time steps. Max time steps maybe H + N? But 10^100 huge. No issue.

Now, let's ensure our algorithm doesn't need to simulate time.

Now, let's consider if there is a known AtCoder problem? This might be AtCoder AGC? The solution likely exactly this segment tree. Good.

Let's think about possible issue with "none of the cells directly below the cells occupied by bar i is occupied by any bar" means if any below cell occupied, bar cannot move. We used that. If instead it required all below cells occupied to stop? No, statement clear: moves if none occupied; otherwise nothing. So any obstacle blocks. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of "lower bars settle independently" in presence of process order. Suppose lower bar B is below upper bar A, but A has smaller index and is processed before B each time. At t=0.5, A checks below cells. If B is directly below at row r_A+1, A blocked. Then B checks below cells. B's below cells are row r_B+1. Could A occupy row r_B+1? No, A row <= r_B-1 (since above B), so no. So B moves if its below empty. Thus A doesn't block B. If A is not directly above B but intersects? If A row < B row, A cannot be at B's below row. So yes. What if A and B intervals intersect but A is not above B in all columns? Since they intersect at some column, and initial R_A < R_B, A remains above B in that column. A's row < B's row always. So A cannot occupy B's below row. Thus B's movement condition independent of A. For any lower bar, all upper bars have smaller rows and cannot occupy its below row. Therefore lower bars' trajectories can be computed without considering upper bars. This is a strong invariant: relative vertical order by initial row for overlapping intervals. Need prove relative order preserved. We can mention but final code only.

Now, let's think about if two bars have overlapping intervals but initial rows not ordered? They have some order. If R_i < R_k, they overlap. At any time, can R_i become equal to R_k? No, because same row overlap would mean collision. Could R_i become greater than R_k? To pass, they'd need cross; impossible because movement is one cell down and blocked by occupied cells. Formal: For any overlapping column c, let rows of bars in that column. The lower bar cannot move into a cell occupied by upper; upper cannot move into lower. Since both move only down, their relative order in that column is invariant. Thus R_i < R_k implies r_i < r_k always. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar's interval intersects multiple lower bars, and the minimum final row q among them may be from a bar that is not "directly below" at the time because there is another bar with same column but higher? Wait if another bar with same column and lower initial row but final row less than q? That would be q. If there is a bar with same column and final row greater than q, it is below the q bar? Actually if same column, vertical order by initial row. Suppose bar k at row q, bar j at row s>q in same column. Then k is above j. When i falls to q-1, below cell at that column is k at q, blocked. So j irrelevant. Good.

Now, let's think about if the minimum q is from a lower bar that intersects I_i but at a column where another bar with even smaller row (higher) also covers? That other bar would have final row < q, making q not min. So no.

Now, let's consider if a bar's final row could be determined by a lower bar that does not intersect its interval but is directly below due to horizontal? Directly below means same column, so must intersect. Good.

Now, let's think about coordinate compression? W up to 2e5, no need.

Let's write final code with iterative lazy segtree.

Potential bug in AtCoder lazy segtree range_apply update conditions. Let's verify thoroughly.

Standard AtCoder LazySegTree (from ac-library-python):
```
def apply(self, l, r, f):
    if l == r: return
    l += self.size
    r += self.size
    for i in range(self.log, 0, -1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l: self._push(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r: self._push((r - 1) >> i)
    l2, r2 = l, r
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            self._all_apply(l, f)
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            self._all_apply(r, f)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    l, r = l2, r2
    for i in range(1, self.log + 1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l: self._update(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r: self._update((r - 1) >> i)
```
Yes.

Range_prod:
```
def prod(self, l, r):
    if l == r: return e
    l += self.size
    r += self.size
    for i in range(self.log, 0, -1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l: self._push(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r: self._push((r - 1) >> i)
    sml = e
    smr = e
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            sml = op(sml, d[l])
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            smr = op(d[r], smr)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    return op(sml, smr)
```
Good.

Need ensure `_push` only called on internal nodes. In push loop, `l >> i` for i from log down to 1. Since l >= size, l>>i >=1 and < size? For i=log, l>>log could be 1 if l<2*size? Actually l in [size,2size), l>>log =1. For i=1, l>>1 in [size/2, size), internal. So okay. `(r-1)>>i` similarly. In `_push`, k < size always. Good.

Now, one subtlety: In `_all_apply`, we set `data[k] = f`. For assignment, if f is 0? no. But if f equals INF? We never assign INF. Good.

Now, let's think about if range_apply assignment to a node that has lazy pending from previous assignment. `_all_apply` overwrites data and lazy. That's correct for assignment: if a segment previously assigned to old value, now assign to new value, children may still have old lazy? Wait if a node has lazy old, its children data may not reflect old. If we now `_all_apply` to the same node with new f, we set data[k]=new f and lazy[k]=new f. Children remain stale old, but that's okay because lazy[k]=new f indicates the entire segment should be new f; when later pushed, children will be overwritten with new f. So no need to clear children. Good.

Now, let's test iterative segtree with a scenario where range_apply partial after previous full assignment:
n=4, assign [0,4) to 5: apply root data=5 lazy=5.
assign [1,3) to 4:
l=5,r=7,l0=5,r0=7.
push loop:
i=2: l0>>2=1, condition true? ((5>>2)<<2)=4 !=5 -> push(1): root lazy=5, apply children node2,node3 data=5 lazy=5, root lazy=0. r0>>2=1, ((7>>2)<<2)=4 !=7 -> push(1) again (lazy 0).
i=1: l0>>1=2, ((5>>1)<<1)=4 !=5 -> push(2): node2 lazy=5, apply leaves4,5 data=5 lazy=5, node2 lazy=0. r0>>1=3, ((7>>1)<<1)=6 !=7 -> push(3): node3 lazy=5, apply leaves6,7 data=5, node3 lazy=0.
while: l=5,r=7. l&1 apply leaf5=4; r&1 r=6 apply leaf6=4. l=3,r=3.
update ancestors:
i=1: l0>>1=2 condition true update node2=min(data4=5,data5=4)=4. r0>>1=3 condition true update node3=min(data6=4,data7=5)=4.
i=2: l0>>2=1 condition true update root=min(node2=4,node3=4)=4.
Query [0,4): l=4,r=8. push loop: i=2: l>>2=1, ((4>>2)<<2)=4 == l no; r>>2=2? r=8, r>>2=2, ((8>>2)<<2)=8 == r no. i=1: l>>1=2, ((4>>1)<<1)=4 == l no; r>>1=4, ((8>>1)<<1)=8 == r no. while l=4,r=8 -> l=2,r=4 -> l=1,r=2 -> r&1? r=2 even; l=1,r=1 end? Let's step: l=4,r=8; l>>=1 =>2,r>>=1=>4; l even r even; l>>=1=>1,r>>=1=>2; l&1 -> sml=data1=4, l=2; r even; l>>=1=>1,r>>=1=>1 end. min=4. Good.

Now, let's think about if range_apply with l=0,r=n where n not power of two. size > n. Leaves beyond n remain INF. Query never includes beyond n. Assign never includes beyond n. But update ancestors may include data from beyond n? For nodes partially covering beyond n, if we assign a range within n, update uses min of children including beyond n leaves INF, so fine. If we assign root? We never assign beyond n because r<=n. So no issue.

Now, let's consider if we can simplify by using recursive segment tree to avoid potential bugs in iterative update conditions. But iterative is standard. I'll use iterative.

Let's write code:

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    H, W, N = map(int, input().split())
    bars = []
    for idx in range(N):
        R, C, L = map(int, input().split())
        bars.append((R, C, L, idx))
    bars.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

    INF = H + 1
    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < W:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1
    data = [INF] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [0] * size

    def update_node(k):
        left = data[k << 1]
        right = data[k << 1 | 1]
        data[k] = left if left < right else right

    def all_apply(k, f):
        data[k] = f
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = f

    def push(k):
        f = lazy[k]
        if f:
            all_apply(k << 1, f)
            all_apply(k << 1 | 1, f)
            lazy[k] = 0

    def range_apply(l, r, f):
        if l >= r:
            return
        l += size
        r += size
        l0, r0 = l, r
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                all_apply(l, f)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                all_apply(r, f)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        l, r = l0, r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                update_node(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                update_node((r - 1) >> i)

    def range_min(l, r):
        if l >= r:
            return INF
        l += size
        r += size
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                push(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                push((r - 1) >> i)
        sml = INF
        smr = INF
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                if data[l] < sml:
                    sml = data[l]
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                if data[r] < smr:
                    smr = data[r]
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        return sml if sml < smr else smr

    ans = [0] * N
    for R, C, L, idx in bars:
        l = C - 1
        r = C + L - 1  # exclusive
        q = range_min(l, r)
        if q == INF:
            row = H
        else:
            row = q - 1
        ans[idx] = row
        range_apply(l, r, row)

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(map(str, ans)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check `r = C + L - 1` exclusive: If C=1,L=1 -> l=0,r=1, covers col0. Good. If C=2,L=3 -> l=1,r=4, covers 1,2,3 (original cols2,3,4). Good.

Now, let's think about if q can be INF but row=H, and then range_apply sets all to H. Later if another bar query includes a column covered by this bar and also an INF column, q=H, row=H-1. Assign all to H-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if q could be H+1 but H maybe 2e5, row=H. If q=H+1, `q == INF` true. Good.

Now, let's think about if there are bars with R descending but same R and one interval disjoint. Suppose same R, but due to sorting, one inserted before another. Could the second's query see the first's assigned value in a column that is not overlapping? No, if disjoint, query interval doesn't include first's columns. If intervals disjoint but adjacent? Query interval [l,r) doesn't include outside. So no. If same R intervals cannot overlap, so safe. If they could be disjoint but one interval contains a column of another? That's overlap. So no.

Now, let's think about if a bar with same R but disjoint interval could still be a lower bar for another same R? No, same R not lower. They don't intersect. So no dependency. Good.

Now, let's consider if initial no overlap but intervals at same row disjoint; our algorithm processes one before another and assigns row. Could the second's query interval include a column assigned by the first due to first's interval extending? That would be overlap, impossible. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof that update is assignment to row for entire interval. We used q = range_min. But range_min returns min cover_min over columns. If q finite, row=q-1. Is row necessarily less than every cover_min[c] in interval? Yes because q is min, so cover_min[c] >= q. row = q-1 < q <= cover_min[c]. If q=INF, cover_min[c]=INF=H+1, row=H < INF. So yes. Thus assignment.

Now, let's think about if there is a column in I_i with cover_min[c] = q but q=INF? If q=INF, all INF. Good.

Now, let's consider if q could be H+1 but some cover_min[c] = H? Then q would be H, not INF. So no.

Now, let's think about if row = q-1 could be equal to some cover_min[c]? No, q-1 < q <= cover_min[c]. So strictly less. Thus every update strictly decreases all values in interval. This monotonicity means each position's value decreases over time. Values are integers from H down to 1. Could a position be updated many times? Yes, up to H maybe, but segment tree handles.

Now, let's consider if the segment tree range_min query after some assignments returns the minimum final row among processed bars covering any column in interval. Because we assign entire interval to row, for a column covered by multiple processed bars, the stored value is the minimum row among them. This is correct for future queries because the highest (smallest row) bar in that column is the one that will block an upper bar. If there is a lower bar with larger row, it is below the highest bar and irrelevant. Good.

Now, let's think about if a future upper bar's interval intersects a processed bar's interval but not at any column where that processed bar is the minimum? Wait if intervals intersect, there is at least one column c in both. At that column, the stored cover_min[c] is at most that processed bar's row (maybe smaller from another bar). Query min over interval will consider c, so the processed bar's row is represented. If another bar with smaller row covers c, then that other bar also intersects the future bar (because it covers c in future interval) and is a higher obstacle, so min should be that smaller row. Good.

Now, let's consider if a processed bar's interval intersects future bar's interval at a column c, but at c there is an even higher processed bar that also intersects future bar. Then min cover_min[c] is the higher bar, which is correct. The lower processed bar doesn't matter. So storing min per column is sufficient.

Now, let's think about if a future bar's interval contains a column c with cover_min[c] from a processed bar that intersects future bar at c, but the processed bar's final row is lower than another processed bar that intersects future bar at a different column. Query min over all columns picks the minimum row among all intersecting bars. That's exactly q. Good.

Now, let's consider if there are bars with same column but different intervals, and a future bar's interval covers a range; query min over range gives min row among all bars covering any column in range. This is min row among all bars whose interval intersects range. Because if a bar intersects range, it covers some column in range, so its row appears at that column's cover_min (or lower). Conversely, any value at a column in range comes from a bar covering that column, so intersects. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a bar with interval [a,b] that intersects query [l,r] but doesn't cover any column where it is the minimum because another bar with smaller row covers the same column. Then the smaller row bar also intersects query, so min is even smaller. So q still correct.

Now, let's consider if the final row of a bar could be less than q-1 due to a bar that is initially above it but falls and lands on top of it, somehow pushing? No pushing; bars don't interact except blocking. A bar above cannot push down. It just rests on top. So no.

Now, let's think about if a bar can be supported by a bar above? No, gravity down; above doesn't support. It only blocks downward if below. So no.

Now, let's consider if a bar with no lower intersecting bars falls to bottom H. But what if there is a lower bar with disjoint interval but at bottom row that occupies a cell diagonally below? Diagonal doesn't block. Condition only directly below same columns. So no.

Now, let's think about if a bar's interval length L_i could be large, up to W. Segment tree range assign O(log W) independent of length. Good.

Now, let's consider if N=2e5, W=2e5, H=2e5, recursion not used. Iterative segtree functions nested with closures might have overhead but okay. We can optimize by binding local variables? Not necessary but could.

Potential issue with Python closures and nested functions: `data`, `lazy`, `size`, `log` are captured; function calls inside loops may be slower. But 2e5 operations * ~2*log* function calls to push/all_apply/update_node. Could be okay. We can inline some operations for speed? Let's estimate: Each range_apply/range_min calls push in loop log times, each push may call all_apply twice. Range apply also calls update_node. Total maybe ~2e5*2* (18 pushes + 18 updates + while) ~ 14M function calls? Actually push calls all_apply, update_node calls. Could be ~50M function calls, maybe high. Need optimize for Python.

We can implement iterative lazy segtree with local functions but still function call overhead. AtCoder Python time maybe 2 sec? Need be careful. We can optimize by using recursive? Recursive also function calls. Maybe use a different data structure exploiting range assignment to lower value to get O(log N) with a heap? Let's explore if there is an even simpler approach.

Because updates are range assignment to a value lower than all values in interval. This is equivalent to maintaining for each column the minimum row of processed bars covering it. Since values only decrease, and each bar's row is determined by min over interval. Could we process bars in descending R and use a priority queue / segment tree? Segment tree is standard.

Maybe we can use a Fenwick tree? Range assign and range min with monotonic decreasing assignments. Since assignment value is lower than all values in interval, after assignment, the interval becomes uniform with the new minimum. This is like maintaining a set of disjoint intervals with constant values (a "Chtholly tree" / ordered disjoint interval tree). If we implement using a sorted list of intervals, operations could be O(number of intervals removed + log). Since each update removes all intervals overlapping [l,r] and replaces with one interval. Each interval inserted once and removed once, so total O(N log N) if we have a balanced BST. Python lacks balanced BST, but we can implement with `bisect` on lists? Insert/delete in list O(N) too slow. Could use `heapq`? Not for intervals.

Segment tree is safest.

Can we optimize iterative segtree by using arrays and loops without nested function calls? We can write functions but maybe okay. Let's consider AtCoder typical 2e5, Python with iterative lazy segtree should pass if optimized. We can reduce function calls by inlining push/all_apply in loops? But code complexity.

Let's estimate more concretely. For each bar:
- range_min: push loop 18 iterations, each may call push if condition true. Conditions often true? For arbitrary l,r, about half? Actually condition checks if l is aligned. It will push ancestors that are not fully covered. In worst case, many. Then while loop ~18, each iteration simple. So ~36 loop iterations + maybe 18 push calls.
- range_apply: similar push loop 18, while 18, update loop 18. So ~72 loop iterations + push/update calls.
Total 2e5*108 ~21.6M loop iterations, plus function calls. Python can handle ~20M in 2-3 seconds maybe okay. Nested function calls could add overhead but still maybe okay. AtCoder time limit unknown. We can optimize by using PyPy.

Could use a recursive segment tree with arrays and local variables; recursion overhead maybe similar.

Let's see if we can implement a simpler segment tree with "range assign and range min" using iterative lazy but with functions defined outside? We'll keep.

Potential bug: In `range_apply`, after applying, we update ancestors using l0/r0. But if l0/r0 conditions false for some i, we don't update that ancestor. Standard. However, if we applied to a node that is an ancestor of l0 or r0-1, the update loop will update ancestors above it. Need ensure if l0/r0 aligned, the node itself may have been updated and its ancestors need update. The condition `((l0 >> i) << i) != l0` means the i-th ancestor of l0 is not completely covered by the update? Let's trust AtCoder.

Condition any occupied -> blocked, so A stops at5. Good. If C final row4? But C R=5 cannot final4. So okay.

Now, let's think about if a lower bar's final row can be less than another lower bar's initial row? No, final row >= initial row. So q >= min R_k > R_i. Good.

Now, let's consider if the process could have a bar move down multiple times in one time step? No, at most one per time. Our final equation ignores timing but okay.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility of "oscillation" where bars move forever? No, each move increases row by 1, bounded H, so finite.

Now, let's consider if the final state after 10^100 is same as stable state. Since total moves finite <= N*H? Actually each bar moves at most H-R_i times, total <= N*H <= 4e10. Each time step can move multiple bars, but number of time steps until no moves could be as large as total moves? Let's see if a bar can move at most once per time step, so total time steps needed could be max over bars of number of moves? Not exactly, because a bar might wait. But total moves finite; after no moves, stable. 10^100 >> 4e10? 10^100 >> 4e10 yes. So enough. Even if time steps needed O(N*H) =4e10, 10^100 much larger. Good.

Now, let's think about if H,W,N all 2e5, total moves could be 4e10, but time 10^100 is fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using INF=H+1 when q=INF and row=H. If H=2e5, INF=200001. If a processed bar has row H=200000, stored value 200000. Query q=200000, row=199999. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bar's final row could be H when q=INF, but if there are lower bars not intersecting, they don't block. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar's interval is large and there is a lower bar with final row q at one column, but another lower bar with final row q+1 at another column. The upper bar stops at q-1 because below q column occupied. It doesn't need all below columns occupied to stop; any occupied blocks. So q min. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bar's interval is large and there is a lower bar with final row q at one column, but at row q-1, the upper bar's interval might overlap with another bar at same row q-1? That would be a collision? Could another bar (not lower) occupy row q-1 in a column of upper bar's interval, preventing upper from moving to q-1? Wait upper bar wants to move down to row q-1. If there is already a bar at row q-1 in one of its columns, then upper cannot move into row q-1 because it would overlap. But can that happen? That bar would be at the same row as upper's target. It could be a bar that is initially above or below? If a bar occupies row q-1 in a column of I_i, and upper i is currently above q-1, then that bar is below i? Actually if it's at row q-1 and i is above, then it is below i. It would have final row q-1, which is < q, contradicting q being min final row among lower intersecting bars. So no such bar at final. Temporarily, there might be a bar at row q-1 that later moves down. It could block i temporarily, but eventually moves. So final i can reach q-1. Good.

What if a bar with initial row above i but final row q-1? It is above i, cannot be at row q-1 below i? If it is above i, its row < r_i. Since i is above q-1? Actually i final q-1, an above bar final row < q-1, so not at q-1. So no.

Thus final equation robust.

Now, let's consider if the segment tree stores min final row among processed bars covering each column. When we assign interval to row, we are effectively saying bar i covers all those columns at row row. But if there is a processed bar with smaller row in a column, we argued impossible because row < q <= all values. So bar i becomes the minimum in every column of its interval. This is important: It means every column in its interval now has cover_min = row. Thus for future bars, if they intersect any part of I_i, they will see row as candidate. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bar's interval is very large and q is from a lower bar at one edge; row=q-1. Assigning row to the entire interval means for columns far from the lower bar, the bar i now covers them at row q-1. Is that physically true? Yes, bar i is a horizontal bar occupying all columns in its interval at row q-1. It covers all those columns. Even if it was blocked by a bar at one column, it still occupies all columns in its interval at that row. So yes.

Now, let's consider if a bar could be blocked by a lower bar at one column but continue moving down if that lower bar moves down later? It will move down later as lower bar moves, but final row q-1 where q is min final row. So yes.

Now, let's think about if a lower bar with final row q might be in a column where another lower bar with final row q' < q also covers, but q' not in I_i? If q' not in I_i, it doesn't intersect i, so cannot block. But if it covers the same column as the q bar and that column is in I_i, then q' would also be in I_i (same column), so q' would be considered. Thus q is min among all lower bars intersecting I_i. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a lower bar with final row q but its interval intersects I_i at a column c; at final, bar i at row q-1, below cell (q,c) occupied by that bar. But what if another bar with smaller row also occupies (q,c)? Impossible, one cell one bar. Good.

Now, let's think about if a lower bar's final row q could be equal to H, and row=H-1. If H=1, no lower bars. Good.

Now, let's consider if the initial no overlap condition is necessary for our sort by R? If two bars same row overlapping, our algorithm would process one then the other and maybe produce invalid, but constraints avoid.

Now, let's think about if there is any need to group by R and process all same R before querying? We argued no because same R intervals disjoint. But wait, what if same R intervals are disjoint, but one same-R bar's assigned value could affect another same-R bar's query if the query interval includes a column covered by the first due to the first's interval being disjoint? No. If disjoint, no common column. So safe. If same R intervals are disjoint but one interval is completely to the left, query interval of the other is to the right, no overlap. So no effect. Thus arbitrary order okay.

Now, let's think about if there are bars with same R and one interval starts where another ends? E.g., bar A [1,2], bar B [3,4], disjoint. Query A [1,2] doesn't include 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar with same R but disjoint interval could be "below" another same R bar in a different column? They are same row, no vertical order. They don't interact. Good.

Now, let's think about if the problem's bars are numbered and process order by i, but our sort by R ignores i. We argued final independent. Let's try to find a counterexample where index order matters due to a lower bar being blocked by an upper bar that moves into a cell below it? We argued impossible. Let's formalize: For any two bars A and B with overlapping columns, if R_A < R_B, then at all times r_A < r_B. Proof by induction: Initially true. At a time step, suppose before step r_A < r_B. If A moves first, it checks cells below; if r_A+1 = r_B and overlap, blocked, so r_A unchanged. If r_A+1 < r_B, A moves to r_A+1, still < r_B. If B moves later, it checks below; A is above, so not below; B may move to r_B+1, maintaining r_A < r_B. If B moves first, similar. Thus invariant. Therefore an upper bar never occupies a cell below a lower bar. So lower bar's movement condition (cells below) cannot be affected by any upper bar. It can be affected only by bars that are below it in overlapping columns, i.e., with larger initial R. Thus bottom-up.

What about bars with disjoint intervals? They never affect each other directly. If they share a column? Disjoint means no. So no.

Thus final independent of index.

Now, let's think about if a lower bar's movement can be affected by an upper bar that has disjoint interval but after moving down, its interval becomes overlapping? Horizontal positions fixed, so no.

Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a more direct formula: final row = min over lower intersecting bars -1, recursively. Our segment tree computes exactly.

Now, let's think about if the segment tree range_min query over interval [l,r) returns INF if no processed bar covers any column. But what if there is a processed bar covering a column but with value INF? We never store INF; all assigned rows <=H. So INF means no coverage. Good.

Now, let's consider if we should initialize data with INF=H+1, but if H=2e5, INF=200001. If a processed bar has row=H=200000, data value 200000. Query q=200000, row=199999. Good.

Now, let's think about if row could be 0 if q=1. We argued no. But let's double-check with H=1: no lower bars, q=INF=2, row=1. If H=2, lower bar initial R=2 final row=2, upper R=1 q=2 row=1. Good. So row>=1.

Now, let's consider if a bar's final row could be less than its initial row due to q from a lower bar with final row less than R_i+1? Lower bar final row >= R_k > R_i, so q >= R_i+1? Actually if R_k = R_i+1, final row >= R_i+1, so q >= R_i+1. Thus row >= R_i. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a lower bar with final row q but q = R_i (same as upper initial)? Impossible because lower final row >= R_k > R_i. Good.

Now, let's consider if the segment tree assignment to row could set a column to a value smaller than the initial row of a future bar? That's fine; it represents an obstacle above that future bar? Wait if a future bar has initial row R_j > row? Actually future bars processed later have smaller R (higher up). So row (final of processed bar) is below them initially? Processed bars have larger R, so they are initially below future bars. Their final row is >= initial R > R_future. So row > R_future. Thus not above future bar. Good.

Now, let's think about if a processed bar's final row could be less than a future bar's initial row? No, processed R > future R, final row >= processed R > future R. So row > future R. Good.

Now, let's consider if the segment tree values are monotonically decreasing over time (processing order). Since each update sets interval to row < all current values in interval, yes. This monotonicity might allow a specialized segment tree but not needed.

Now, let's think about if range_apply with row=H when q=INF sets interval to H. If later another bar with same R? Same R processed maybe after; if disjoint, no effect. If same R and disjoint, no overlap. If same R and overlapping impossible. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bar with R very small and no lower bars, row=H, assign H. Then another bar with even smaller R? Actually sorted descending, after processing small R, future bars have even smaller R. They will see this bar at H. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bar with R=1 and no lower bars row=H. Good.

Example: H=4, W=3, bars:
1: R=1, C=1, L=3 (interval [1,3])
2: R=2, C=1, L=1 ([1])
3: R=3, C=3, L=1 ([3])
No overlap? Bar1 row1 [1,3], bar2 row2 [1], bar3 row3 [3]. They overlap with bar1 but not each other? bar2 [1] and bar3 [3] disjoint.
Algorithm:
Process R=3 bar3: q INF row4 assign col3=4.
R=2 bar2: query col1 INF row4 assign col1=4.
R=1 bar1: query [1,3] min(4, INF? col2 INF, col3=4) =4 row3 assign all 3.
Outputs: bar1=3, bar2=4, bar3=4.
Simulate:
t0: bar1 row1 [1,3], bar2 row2 [1], bar3 row3 [3].
t0.5 order 1,2,3:
bar1 below row2 col1 occupied by bar2, blocked.
bar2 below row3 col1 empty? bar3 at col3, so empty -> moves to3.
bar3 below row4 col3 empty -> moves to4.
t1.5:
bar1 below row2 col1 empty? bar2 at3, col2 empty, col3 empty? bar3 at4, so all empty -> moves to2.
bar2 below row4 col1 empty -> moves to4.
bar3 bottom.
t2.5:
bar1 below row3 col1 occupied by bar2? bar2 at4, so row3 col1 empty; col2 empty; col3 empty? bar3 at4, row3 col3 empty. So bar1 moves to3.
bar2 bottom, bar3 bottom.
t3.5:
bar1 below row4 col1 occupied by bar2, col2 empty, col3 occupied by bar3 -> blocked. Final bar1=3. Good.

Wait bar1 at2, below row3: col1 empty, col2 empty, col3 empty? bar4 at5, so row3 col3 empty. So bar1 moves to3. bar2 below row5 col1 occupied by bar3, blocked.
t4.5: bar1 below row4 col1 occupied by bar2, blocked. Final bar1=3. Good.

Let's verify iterative lazy segtree with range_apply and range_min in the context of assignment to lower value. There is a known issue: In range_apply, if we apply to a node, we set data[k]=f and lazy[k]=f. But if the node has children with values smaller than f? We argued f is smaller than all current values in the range, so no child should have smaller. But due to lazy, data[k] is min of segment. If f < data[k], then f < all values? Wait data[k] is min of segment, so if f < data[k], then f is smaller than the minimum, hence smaller than all values in segment. So assignment is safe. But does our range_apply know that f < data[k] for the nodes it applies to? It applies to nodes fully covered by [l,r). Since q = range_min(l,r) and f = q-1 (or H if q=INF). For any fully covered node, its data[k] >= q? Actually data[k] is min over that subsegment. Since subsegment is within [l,r), data[k] >= q (because q is min over whole interval). If q=INF, data[k]=INF. Thus f < q <= data[k]. So f < data[k]. Therefore assignment to f is indeed a decrease for every such node. This property ensures lazy assignment doesn't need to preserve smaller children. Good.

Now, let's think about if q=INF but some data[k] in interval is INF and f=H. f < INF. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bar with lower intersecting bar but that lower bar is supported by another bar that is not in the upper bar's interval. Example upper A [1,2], lower B [2,2], lower C [1,1] below B? Actually C below B but C interval [1,1] not intersect A? It intersects A at col1, so it is also lower intersecting. If C supports B? What if B is supported by a bar D that does not intersect A? For B to be supported by D, D must be below B and intersect B. If D intersects B at col2, then D also intersects A (since A contains col2). So any support of B in a column of A also intersects A. If B's support is in a column not in A? B's interval may extend outside A; but B intersects A at some column. B could be supported by a bar D that only intersects B outside A, while the part of B inside A is supported by another bar E? Wait a bar B is blocked if any cell below is occupied. It doesn't need support under every column; any support blocks. So B's final row is determined by the highest obstacle in any column of B, which could be outside A. If that obstacle is outside A, it doesn't directly intersect A, but it determines B's row. Then A's q includes B's row because B intersects A. So A stops above B, even though B's support is outside A. Is that physically correct? Let's examine.

Example: H=10. A interval [1,2], R=1. B interval [2,3], R=2. B intersects A at col2. B is blocked by D interval [3,3], R=3, which is outside A (col3). D final maybe bottom 10. B final row = 9 (q from D). A query sees B row 9 (and maybe no other), q=9, A row=8. Simulate: B falls until above D at row9? D at bottom10, B at9. A falls until above B at row8. Even though B's blocking support D is outside A, B itself occupies col2 at row9, so A blocked at row8. Correct. So q includes B's row, regardless of why B stopped. Good.

What if B's row is determined by a bar outside A, but B's interval intersects A at a column where there is no obstacle below B; B still occupies that column at row q, so A blocked. Good.

Thus storing final row of B is enough.

Now, let's think about if there is a bar B that intersects A but B is initially above A? Then B is not processed as lower; it won't be in segment tree when A processed. Could B later fall and become below A? No, order preserved. So it won't block A. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar B initially above A but with disjoint interval initially? If disjoint, never intersects. If after movement? Horizontal fixed, so no.

Now, let's think about if a bar B initially above A but intervals intersect, B remains above A, so cannot block A. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar B initially below A but intervals intersect, B remains below A, so can block A. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bar B initially below A but intervals intersect only at a column where B is not below because B's interval starts after A's ends? Intersect means some column in both. At that column, B is below. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a bar B initially below A but B's interval intersects A, but B moves down and A moves down; could A ever pass B? No. Good.

Now, let's consider if the segment tree query over interval [l,r) returns min cover_min, but cover_min[c] is min final row among processed bars covering c. If a processed bar covers c but its final row is not the minimum because another processed bar covers c with smaller row, the smaller row bar is also below A? It covers c, and c is in A interval, so it intersects A. It is processed (lower R). Its row is smaller, so it is a higher obstacle. Thus q should be that smaller row. So storing min is correct.

Now, let's think about if a processed bar covers c but its interval intersects A at c, but its initial R is lower? Processed bars have R > current R, so lower. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a processed bar with R > current R but its interval intersects A at c, but due to order preservation, is it guaranteed to be below A at final? Yes, because initial R > current R and they overlap at c, so relative order in that column remains. Good.

Now, let's think about if a processed bar with R > current R but interval intersects A at c, but another bar with even smaller row covers c and is above it; the processed bar might be below that other bar, but still below A? Since A is above all processed bars (R_A < R_processed), and order preserved, A remains above them. So yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a bar with R > current R but interval intersects A, but its final row is computed using bars that are above it? We argued lower bars independent, so final row correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the segment tree update assigning row to entire interval could inadvertently erase information about a lower bar with smaller row in a column? We argued row < all current values, so no lower bar with smaller row exists in interval. What about a lower bar with smaller row outside interval? Not affected. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar's interval is assigned row, and later another bar with even smaller R queries a subinterval; it sees row. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a bar with R descending but not all bars with larger R processed before because sort by R descending; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a bar with R=H and another with R=H (same bottom row) disjoint. Process one row H assign; process other query disjoint no effect row H. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a bar with R=H and another with R=H-1 that overlaps. Process bottom row H, then upper q=H row H-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bar with R=H-1 and another with R=H that overlap, but the upper has smaller index and is processed first in actual time? Our algorithm processes bottom first regardless of index. We argued index irrelevant. Let's simulate to be absolutely sure with a case where upper index smaller:
H=3, W=1. Bar1 (upper) R=1, Bar2 (bottom) R=2? Actually bottom H=3? Let's set H=3, Bar1 R=1, Bar2 R=2. Index upper=1, lower=2. Algorithm: lower row3, upper q=3 row2. Simulate:
t0: bar1 row1, bar2 row2.
t0.5: bar1 below row2 occupied by bar2 -> blocked. bar2 below row3 empty -> moves to3.
t1.5: bar1 below row2 empty -> moves to2. bar2 bottom.
Final upper2. Good.
If lower index smaller: Bar1 lower R=2, Bar2 upper R=1. Algorithm same. Simulate:
t0.5: bar1 (lower) below row3 empty -> moves to3. bar2 (upper) below row2 empty? bar1 moved to3, so yes -> moves to2. Final upper2. Good.

Now, a chain where upper index smaller can delay? Example H=5, bars top R=1 idx1, middle R=2 idx2, bottom R=3 idx3. Simulated earlier final 3,4,5. If indices reversed: top idx3, middle idx2, bottom idx1. Simulate:
t0.5: bottom idx1 moves to4, middle idx2 below row3 empty? bottom at4, so moves to3, top idx3 below row2 empty? middle at3, so moves to2. t1.5: bottom moves to5, middle below row4 empty moves to4, top below row2? top at2, below row3 empty? middle at4, so moves to3. t2.5: bottom bottom, middle below row5 occupied blocked, top below row4 occupied by middle blocked. Final top3, middle4, bottom5. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if a lower bar with larger index can be blocked by an upper bar that moves into its below cell? We argued impossible. In the above simulation, top moved into row2 while bottom at4, not below bottom. Good.

Now, let's consider if there are multiple columns and an upper bar could move into a cell below a lower bar in a column where they don't overlap? If they don't overlap, not directly below. If they overlap, upper remains above. Good.

Now, let's think about if a lower bar's below cell in a column could be occupied by an upper bar that is in a different row but same column? Same column means they overlap; upper row < lower row, so upper cannot be at lower row+1. Good.

Now, let's consider if a bar's movement condition checks cells directly below all cells it occupies. If an upper bar is at row r_lower+1 in a column not occupied by lower bar, then it's not directly below lower bar. So irrelevant.

Thus bottom-up independent.

Now, let's think about if the final stable condition could be characterized by "each bar is either at bottom or has at least one occupied cell directly below". Our equation gives that. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that a bar stops above an obstacle that later moves down, then the bar moves down, so final row is based on final obstacle positions. Our recursive equation accounts.

Now, let's think about if the process order could cause a bar to stop above an obstacle that would have moved down if it had been processed earlier in the same time step, but due to order it is processed after and misses the move for one time step. But over infinite time, it will move next time. So final unaffected.

Actually events at 0.5+n for non-negative integer n. 10^100 = 0.5 + (10^100 - 0.5) not integer, so no event exactly at t=10^100. The state at t=10^100 is after all events with n < 10^100 -0.5, i.e., up to n=10^100-1? Since 10^100 is huge. Stable state. Good.

Now, let's consider if in `range_apply`, the push loop pushes ancestors that are not fully covered. This ensures that when we later apply to nodes, their ancestors' lazy is cleared so we don't lose information. Standard.

Let's maybe consider if we can simplify by using a recursive segment tree to avoid the complex iterative update conditions. But iterative is fine. Let's ensure no syntax errors:
```
def update_node(k):
    left = data[k << 1]
    right = data[k << 1 | 1]
    data[k] = left if left < right else right
```
Operator precedence: `k << 1 | 1` is `(k << 1) | 1` because shift has higher precedence than bitwise OR? In Python, shifts have higher precedence than bitwise AND? Actually precedence: shifts lower than addition, higher than bitwise AND? Bitwise OR has lower precedence than shifts. So `k << 1 | 1` = `(k << 1) | 1`. Good. But for clarity, use `(k << 1) | 1`.

In `_all_apply`:
```
def all_apply(k, f):
    data[k] = f
    if k < size:
        lazy[k] = f
```
Good.

In `push`:
```
def push(k):
    f = lazy[k]
    if f:
        all_apply(k << 1, f)
        all_apply((k << 1) | 1, f)
        lazy[k] = 0
```
Good.

In `range_apply`:
```
def range_apply(l, r, f):
    if l >= r:
        return
    l += size
    r += size
    l0, r0 = l, r
    for i in range(log, 0, -1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            push((r0 - 1) >> i)
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            all_apply(l, f)
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            all_apply(r, f)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    l, r = l0, r0
    for i in range(1, log + 1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
            update_node(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
            update_node((r - 1) >> i)
```
Good.

In `range_min`:
```
def range_min(l, r):
    if l >= r:
        return INF
    l += size
    r += size
    for i in range(log, 0, -1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
            push(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
            push((r - 1) >> i)
    sml = INF
    smr = INF
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            if data[l] < sml:
                sml = data[l]
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            if data[r] < smr:
                smr = data[r]
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    return sml if sml < smr else smr
```
Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if the segment tree's
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
with code.</think>```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W, N = map(int, input().split())

    bars = []
    for idx in range(N):
        R, C, L = map(int, input().split())
        bars.append((R, C, L, idx))

    bars.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

    INF = H + 1

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < W:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    data = [INF] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [0] * size

    def update_node(k):
        left = data[k << 1]
        right = data[(k << 1) | 1]
        data[k] = left if left < right else right

    def all_apply(k, f):
        data[k] = f
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] = f

    def push(k):
        f = lazy[k]
        if f:
            all_apply(k << 1, f)
            all_apply((k << 1) | 1, f)
            lazy[k] = 0

    def range_apply(l, r, f):
        if l >= r:
            return
        l += size
        r += size
        l0, r0 = l, r

        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)

        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                all_apply(l, f)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                all_apply(r, f)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1

        l, r = l0, r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                update_node(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                update_node((r - 1) >> i)

    def range_min(l, r):
        if l >= r:
            return INF
        l += size
        r += size

        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                push(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                push((r - 1) >> i)

        sml = INF
        smr = INF

        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                if data[l] < sml:
                    sml = data[l]
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                if data[r] < smr:
                    smr = data[r]
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1

        return sml if sml < smr else smr

    ans = [0] * N

    for R, C, L, idx in bars:
        l = C - 1
        r = C + L - 1

        q = range_min(l, r)
        row = q - 1

        ans[idx] = row
        range_apply(l, r, row)

    print("\n".join(map(str, ans)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
